M\in\text{Sym}_{q}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.
We say that
M
is bipartite if
there exists some
A\in\mathbb{R}^{q_{1}\times q_{2}}

for some
q_{1}+q_{2}=q
, such that

M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbf{0}&A\\
A^{\tt{T}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Lemma 116
.

Let
M\in\text{Sym}_{4}^{\tt{F}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})

be bipartite.
Then
\PlEVAL(M)
is
\#
P-hard unless

M
is isomorphic to
A\otimes B
for some

A,B\in\text{Sym}_{2}^{\tt{F}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})

such that
A_{11}=A_{22}
, and
B_{11}=B_{22}
,
in which case,
\PlEVAL(M)
is also polynomial time tractable.

Proof.

We may assume that
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbf{0}&A\\
A^{\tt{T}}&\mathbf{0}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
for some
A\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{q_{1}\times q_{2}}
,
where
q_{1}+q_{2}=4
.
If
q_{1}=1
(or
q_{2}=1
),
we can see that
M
has rank at most
2
.
So, it is not possible that
M\in\text{Sym}_{4}^{\tt{F}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.

So, we may assume that
q_{1}=q_{2}=2
.
Then,
M
must be (upto some isomorphism) of the form

M=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&M_{13}&M_{14}\\
0&0&M_{23}&M_{24}\\
M_{13}&M_{23}&0&0\\
M_{14}&M_{24}&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}

But then, we see that
(T_{n}M)^{2}=A_{n}\oplus A_{n}
, where

A_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}(M_{13})^{2n}+(M_{14})^{2n}&(M_{13}M_{23})^{n}+(M_{14}M_{24})^{n}\\
(M_{13}M_{23})^{n}+(M_{14}M_{24})^{n}&(M_{23})^{2n}+(M_{24})^{2n}\\
\end{pmatrix}

If there exists some
n\geq 1
such that

(A_{n})_{11}\neq(A_{n})_{22}
, then
from
Theorem

100
and

Lemma

114
, it follows that

\PlEVAL((T_{n}M)^{2})\leq\PlEVAL(M)
is
\#
P-hard.

If
(A_{n})_{11}=(A_{n})_{22}
for all
n\geq 1
, that
implies that
\{M_{13},M_{14}\}=\{M_{23},M_{24}\}
as multi-sets.
But if
M_{13}=M_{23}
, and
M_{14}=M_{24}
, then

M
can once again, not be full rank.
This implies that
M_{13}=M_{24}
, and
M_{14}=M_{23}
.
But in this case, we see that

M=B\otimes A
, where
B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
In this case, we see from
Theorem

100

that
\PlEVAL(A)
and
\PlEVAL(B)
are both polynomial
time tractable.
Since
Z_{M}(G)=Z_{A}(G)\cdot Z_{B}(G)
for all
graphs
G=(V,E)
, this implies that

\PlEVAL(M)
is also polynomial time tractable.
∎

This lets us prove the following dichotomy theorem.

Theorem 117
.

Let
M\in\text{Sym}_{4}^{\tt{F}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
.
Then
\PlEVAL(M)
is
\#
P-hard, unless
one of the following conditions is true, in which case,

\PlEVAL(M)
is polynomial tractable.

\displaystyle\mbox{(1)\penalty\ \penalty\ }(\text{direct sum})\penalty\ M\cong A\oplus B\text{ for polynomially tractable }\PlEVAL(A),\PlEVAL(B),

\displaystyle\mbox{(2)\penalty\ \penalty\ }(\text{tensor product})\penalty\ M\cong A\otimes B\text{ for polynomially tractable }\PlEVAL(A),\PlEVAL(B).

Proof.

If
M
is domain separable, i.e.,
M\cong A\oplus B
for any
A,B
, we know from

Lemma

114
that

\PlEVAL(M)
is
\#
P-hard, unless

\PlEVAL(A)
, and
\PlEVAL(B)
are both polynomially tractable,
in which case,
\PlEVAL(M)
is also polynomially
tractable.
If
M
is bipartite, we know from

Lemma

116
that
\PlEVAL(M)
is

\#
P-hard, unless
M\cong A\otimes B
, where

\PlEVAL(A)
, and
\PlEVAL(B)
are both polynomially tractable,
in which case,
\PlEVAL(M)
is also polynomially tractable.

Now, we consider any
M
that is neither
domain separable, nor bipartite.
The underlying graph of
M
where an edge
(i,j)
exists iff
M_{ij}>0

is connected and non-bipartite.
In this case, since

M\in\text{Sym}_{4}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
,
there exists some
n^{*}\geq 1
such that
M^{n}\in\text{Sym}_{4}^{\tt{F}}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
for all

n\geq n^{*}
.
We let
n\geq n^{*}
be some odd integer.
From
Theorem

112
,
we know that
\PlEVAL(M^{n})\leq\PlEVAL(M)
is

\#
P-hard, unless
M^{n}
is isomorphic to

A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}
, for some
polynomial time tractable
\PlEVAL(A^{\prime}),\PlEVAL(B^{\prime})
,
where
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\in\text{Sym}_{2}^{\tt{F}}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
, such that

(A^{\prime})_{11}=(A^{\prime})_{22}
, and
(B^{\prime})_{11}=(B^{\prime})_{22}
.

Without loss of generality, we will now consider the case
where
M^{n}=A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}
for some

A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\in\text{Sym}_{2}^{\tt{F}}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
.
Since
(A^{\prime})_{11}=(A^{\prime})_{22}
, and

(B^{\prime})_{11}=(B^{\prime})_{22}
, we know that

A^{\prime}=HD^{\prime}_{1}H^{\tt{T}}
, and

B^{\prime}=HD^{\prime}_{2}H^{\tt{T}}
, where

H=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\\
\end{pmatrix},D^{\prime}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}_{11}+A^{\prime}_{12}&0\\
0&A^{\prime}_{11}-A^{\prime}_{12}\\
\end{pmatrix},D^{\prime}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}B^{\prime}_{11}+B^{\prime}_{12}&0\\
0&B^{\prime}_{11}-B^{\prime}_{12}\\
\end{pmatrix}

So, if we let
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}=A^{\prime}_{11}+A^{\prime}_{12}
,

\lambda_{2}^{\prime}=A^{\prime}_{11}-A^{\prime}_{12}
,

\mu_{1}^{\prime}=B^{\prime}_{11}+B^{\prime}_{12}
, and

\mu_{2}^{\prime}=B^{\prime}_{11}-B^{\prime}_{12}
, we see that

M^{n}=A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}=(H\otimes H)D^{\prime}(H\otimes H)^{\tt{T}}
, where

D^{\prime}=\text{diag}(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime})
.
We also note that since
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\in\text{Sym}_{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
,

\lambda_{1}^{\prime}>|\lambda_{2}^{\prime}|
, and

\mu_{1}^{\prime}>|\mu_{2}^{\prime}|
.
So,
|\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime}|>|\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime}|>|\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime}|
, and

|\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime}|>|\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime}|>|\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime}|
.
If
\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime}\neq\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime}
,
then
M^{n}
would have four distinct eigenvalues.
Otherwise, it will have three distinct eigenvalues,
with one of them being repeated.

Since
A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}=M^{n}
, we may assume that the
eigenvalues of
M
are:
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\nu_{4}
,
such that

(\nu_{1})^{n}=\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime}
,

(\nu_{2})^{n}=\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime}
,

(\nu_{3})^{n}=\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime}
,

(\nu_{4})^{n}=\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime}
.
Since
n
is an odd integer, and

\nu_{i},\lambda_{i}^{\prime},\mu_{i}^{\prime}
are all real,
this implies that

\nu_{1}=(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{n}}
,

\nu_{2}=(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{n}}
,

\nu_{3}=(\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{n}}
,

\nu_{4}=(\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{n}}
.
Now, if we let
\lambda_{i}=(\lambda_{i}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{n}}
,
and
\mu_{i}=(\mu_{i}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{n}}

for
i\in[2]
, we find that

\nu_{1}=\lambda_{1}\mu_{1}
,

\nu_{2}=\lambda_{1}\mu_{2}
,

\nu_{3}=\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}
,

\nu_{4}=\lambda_{2}\mu_{2}
.

Since
M^{n}\cdot M=M\cdot M^{n}
, we know that

M
and
M^{n}
can be simultaneously diagonalized by the same
orthogonal matrix.
Now, if
\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime}\neq\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime}
,
we know that the diagonalizing orthogonal matrix for
M^{n}
is essentially
unique, whose columns are the unit column eigenvectors of
M^{n}

corresponding to the respective eigenvalues. This is
the matrix
(H\otimes H)K
, where

K=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pm 1&0&0&0\\
0&\pm 1&0&0\\
0&0&\pm 1&0\\
0&0&0&\pm 1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Note that for any diagonal matrix
D^{\prime}
, we have
KD^{\prime}=D^{\prime}K
.
This implies that
M=(HD_{1}H^{\tt{T}})\otimes(HD_{2}H^{\tt{T}})
, where

D_{1}=\text{diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, and

D_{2}=\text{diag}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
.
On the other hand, if

\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime}=\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime}
, we see that

(\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{n}}=(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime})^{\frac{1}{n}}
.
So,
\nu_{3}=\lambda_{2}\mu_{1}=\lambda_{1}\mu_{2}=\nu_{2}
as well.
Therefore, we have the following orthogonal decomposition of

\mathbb{R}^{4}=V_{1}\oplus V_{23}\oplus V_{4}
as a direct sum:

V_{1}
(respectively,
V_{4}
) is a one-dimensional
eigenspace corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\mu_{1}^{\prime}

(respectively,
\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\mu_{2}^{\prime}
) of

M^{n}
which is also a one-dimensional
eigenspace corresponding to the eigenvalue
\nu_{1}=\lambda_{1}\mu_{1}
(respectively,

\nu_{4}=\lambda_{2}\mu_{2}
) of

M
;
V_{23}
is a two-dimensional
eigenspace where both
M^{n}
and
M
act as scalar matrices.
Thus any orthogonal matrix that diagonalizes
M^{n}
also diagonalizes
M
.
So, once again, we see that the matrix
M
can be
diagonalized by
H\otimes H
.
So, in either case, we find that

M=(HD_{1}H^{\tt{T}})\otimes(HD_{2}H^{\tt{T}})
, where

D_{1}=\text{diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, and

D_{2}=\text{diag}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
.

If we let
A=HD_{1}H^{\tt{T}}
, and

B=HD_{2}H^{\tt{T}}
, we see that

M=A\otimes B
.
Moreover, we find that

A_{11}=\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})=A_{22}
,
and
B_{11}=\frac{1}{2}(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})=B_{22}
.
So, we see that when

M^{n}=A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}
, where

(A^{\prime})_{11}=(A^{\prime})_{22}
, and
(B^{\prime})_{11}=(B^{\prime})_{22}
, then

M=A\otimes B
for some
A,B
such that

A_{11}=A_{22}
, and
B_{11}=B_{22}
.
Moreover, in this case, it is easily seen that

\PlEVAL(M)
is polynomially tractable.
∎
